A multi-agent fire environment with a hazard-blind reward, and a pre-registered test of training on co-occurring versus isolated stressors

In the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE), structural collapse can add ignitions to a cellular-automaton fire, the fire’s smoke attenuates what agents perceive, and inter-agent communication can be denied; the fire itself optimizes against no one and enters no reward, cost or constraint channel.

Appendices

We introduce the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE), a pure-JAX multi-agent reinforcement-learning environment in which a cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode, structural collapse can add ignitions to it, and its smoke attenuates the agents’ perception, alongside an independent communication-denial axis. The fire is an ambient hazard: a fixed stochastic process with nothing in it optimizing against the agents, carried by no cost or constraint channel, and invisible to the reward, which reads only task variables and agent deaths and prices every death the same whatever its cause. Severity levels are placed relative to a measured percolation threshold, and each stressor is a parameter of one transition kernel, so ablations are bitwise-exact nested models; we also describe a measurement protocol in which reproducibility floors are measured per metric, per GPU and per artifact. As predicted before measurement, the supercritical fire consumes the fuel that collapse would ignite: under trained policies, collapse-seeded ignitions per episode fall 4.8-foldCA-4 from low to high severity. We then pre-register a test of whether training on co-occurring stressors beats training on each in isolation, at a held-out severity and matched compute, with 72DES-3 seeds per arm (above the registered seed ladder’s cap of 60DES-1, a recorded deviation made before unblinding). Joint training raises survival by 0.96RES-2 points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [0.47, 1.44]RES-2); task completion shows no detectable change (−1.73RES-1 points, CI [−4.12, +0.66]RES-1). Neither joint-versus-isolated difference keeps a stable sign over the final halfRES-11 of training: the survival gap opens only in the last fifthRES-12 of the budget, and completion leans negative from 70 %RES-13 of it onward. The survival gain does not survive an inert-share correction registered before unblinding. Because communication denial is inert under our frozen random-projection message encoder at this scale, a thirdDES-8 of the isolated arm’s training episodes contain no active stressor. Removing them, in a 20DES-13-seed sensitivity arm, raises isolated training’s survival by 0.98RES-7 points; joint training then differs from it by −0.03RES-6 points (CI [−0.68, +0.63]RES-6), although it still sees each coupling twiceDES-14 as often and always in combination. Studies of compositional generalization should therefore match the share of training that contains an active stressor before attributing a gain to composition.

1 Introduction

When a training distribution has to cover several stressors, its designer chooses whether the policy meets them together or one at a time, and the choice matters most when they interact. Ecology studies the interactive and cumulative effects of multiple stressors [1][2], and climate science studies compound events and their risk in their own right [3][4]. In reinforcement learning the choice is a question of zero-shot generalization [5]: must a policy see stressors co-occur in training to cope with their co-occurrence at test time? Gao et al. (2024) [6] studied it for imitation-learned manipulation; we study it for survival in multi-agent reinforcement learning.

Where the hazard sits in the learning problem decides what such a comparison can teach. In some fire-related work the hazard is the task, as for aerial agents rewarded for moving to burning trees on a fire's boundary [7]; safe reinforcement learning typically places it in a cost or a constraint [8]. In the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE) it does neither. The fire is an ambient hazard (Definition 2, §3): a fixed stochastic process with nothing in it optimizing against the agents, carried by no cost or constraint channel and invisible to the reward, which reads task variables and agent deaths and prices every death at \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause. Such a hazard is not new in itself; what it gives this study is a comparison in which no reward or cost signal mentions the hazard in either arm. Proposition 1 shows that the fire, which looks like nonstationarity to any one agent, is state: CHE is a well-defined Dec-POMDP, whose states reachable within the horizon form a finite set (§3). With communication denied it does not reduce to a multi-agent POMDP, and finite-horizon Dec-POMDPs are NEXP-complete [9], so we learn policies rather than plan (Remark 1).

Figure 1 shows one episode. CHE is written in pure JAX, and a cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode. For the idealized kernel the burnt region is exactly a bond-percolation cluster (Proposition 2) [10][11], so the threeCAL-3 severities are values of the fire's transmissibility β placed below, near and above a measured percolation threshold of the implemented kernel (§4). Two stressors are coupled to the fire: collapse can add ignitions (Coupling A, of strength \(\kappa_A\); §5), and smoke attenuates each agent's egocentric observation by Beer–Lambert transmittance (Coupling B, \(\kappa_B\); §6). A third, link denial at level δ (§7), is independent of it; \(\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)\) is the environment configuration. Each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel whose random numbers are drawn whether or not it is on, so switching it off reproduces the nested model's trajectories bit for bit under the same random keys (§3).

The training question becomes a pre-registered test of two training mixtures (Figure 2).

An isolated arm (ISO) trains one policy on single-stressor configurations and a joint arm (JOINT) one on configurations with every stressor on, both at β = 0.43CAL-3 and 0.70CAL-3; both are evaluated at θ*, every stressor on at the near-critical β = 0.49CAL-3, which neither saw. Architecture, hyperparameters, evaluation draw and compute (1000DES-13 updates per run) are matched, with one shared-parameter IPPO policy per training seedENV-11 [12]. The estimand is the compositional gap Γ, JOINT's mean minus ISO's at θ*, on two co-primary outcomes, task completion and survival rate, Šidák-corrected for m = 2DES-13 tests. FourDES-12 outcome branches, a framing for each and a falsifier for the asymmetric outcome were registered on 2026-08-13DES-12, before the grid began (a fifthDX-102 outcome cell was added before unblinding, Appendix E). We ran 72DES-3 seeds per arm, above the registered seed ladder's cap of 60DES-1, a recorded deviation made before unblinding (§9.1).

Figure 2 also shows two weaknesses, stated here before the result. First, JOINT sees each coupling three timesDES-7 as often as ISO, so the endpoints differ in frequency as well as in co-occurrence. A secondary sweep in the spirit of DBCA [13] holds each coupling's frequency fixed while co-occurrence varies, but on the simplex of episode types it cannot also hold the fire-only share fixed, which rises with co-occurrenceDES-9, and it is non-verdict-bearing (§9). Second, communication denial is inert under our frozen random-projection message encoder at this scale (§7), so the thirdDES-8 of ISO's episodes that carry only δ are fire-only. The active share, the fraction of training episodes with at least one behaviourally active stressor, is therefore two thirdsDES-14 for ISO and oneDES-14 for JOINT. Figure 3 gives the registered readings at θ*.

At θ* the founding hypothesis, that joint training improves both outcomes, did not occur (Figure 3). Joint training raises survival by 0.96RES-2 points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [0.47, 1.44]RES-2; points are rate differences × 100), and the survival test rejects. Task completion shows no detectable change, −1.73RES-1 points (CI [−4.12, +0.66]RES-1), which excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66RES-1 points. The falsifier registered for an asymmetric outcome passes (§9.2), so the asymmetry stands. Under Definition 2 it lies between a penalized channel, deaths, and a rewarded one, the task. Read over training, both co-primaries are sign unstable over the final halfRES-11: the survival gap opens only in the last fifthRES-12 of the budget, and completion leans negative from 70 %RES-13 of it onward (Figure 12).

The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. The fire-only third was registered as a bias, with ISO-4 to remove it, on 2026-09-18DES-15, mid-grid and before unblinding; ISO-4 is ISO without its δ-only components, a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family with 20DES-13 seeds. Its survival is 0.98RES-7 points above ISO's, and Γ₄, JOINT minus ISO-4, is −0.03RES-6 points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [−0.68, +0.63]RES-6), and a same-card secondary replicate, proposed after Γ₄ was seen, agrees (§9.2). As the registration requires, we do not describe the survival advantage as a composition effect. Two limits apply. Γ₄'s upper bound, +0.63RES-6, lies above Γ's lower bound, +0.47RES-2: the correction excludes a large composition effect, not every one. And ISO-4 raises both the active share (from two thirdsDES-14 to oneDES-14) and each coupling's frequency (from a thirdDES-7 to a halfDES-14), so its rise cannot say by itself which matters; §9.2 reports what the non-verdict-bearing secondary design adds, its slope on the fire-only share and where its plane fit fails. Once the active share is matched, JOINT's remaining differences add nothing detectable to survival, although it still sees each coupling twiceDES-14 as often as ISO-4 and always in combination.

The argument is general, though measured in one environment: a joint-versus-isolated comparison that leaves the active share free can report an active-share effect as a composition effect. In the search described in §2, which read abstracts and did not query IEEE Xplore, we found no reinforcement-learning comparison that matches its arms on that share. We claim no convergence guarantee and no theorem that joint training wins: Proposition 4 bounds what isolated training can be guaranteed to transfer, not what it does, and Theorem 1 concerns adaptivity against memorization in a two-corridor toy. Nor do we claim an exact critical value for the implemented kernel, or a survival gap robust to the training budget: every comparison is at matched compute, none at convergence.

Our contributions are:

  1. An environment built as an instrument (§§3–7): a lethal fire confined to the transition and observation kernels (Definition 2), each step factorized into collapse, fire, smoke, agents and communication (Proposition 1), severity by measured dynamical phase, bitwise-exact nested ablations, and theory that doubles as unit tests (Proposition 3, Theorem 1, Remark 2′).
  2. Calibration findings that stand alone (§§5–7). Under trained policies, collapse-seeded ignitions fall 4.8-foldCA-4 from low to high severity, as the Coupling-A lock had recorded from the random-policy calibration before any trained policy was measured (§5), while their share near agents stays flatCA-6. Smoke costs survival at high severity (the same direction in three of threeCB-5 replicates; masking concentrated 16–118-foldCB-10 on moments of danger), but measured −0.03CB-9 points at the test's medium severity, graded only against a three-seedCB-9 spread. Communication is inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale.
  3. A measurement protocol in which acceptance bars come with floors (§8): floors per metric, per GPU and per artifact; contrasts graded on their own standard error; design-stage power as the minimum effect detectable with 80 %METH-7 power at the family-corrected α; the bars without a floor named.
  4. A pre-registered test of joint against isolated training (§9), whose survival advantage does not survive a correction registered before unblinding, and a rule whose argument is general: match the active share before attributing a gain to composition.

## 1 Introduction

When a training distribution has to cover several stressors, its designer chooses whether the policy meets them together or one at a time, and the choice matters most when they interact. Ecology studies the interactive and cumulative effects of multiple stressors <d-cite key="crain2008stressors"></d-cite><d-cite key="cote2016stressors"></d-cite>, and climate science studies compound events and their risk in their own right <d-cite key="zscheischler2018compound"></d-cite><d-cite key="zscheischler2020typology"></d-cite>. In reinforcement learning the choice is a question of zero-shot generalization <d-cite key="kirk2023zsg"></d-cite>: must a policy see stressors co-occur in training to cope with their co-occurrence at test time? Gao et al. (2024) <d-cite key="gao2024composition"></d-cite> studied it for imitation-learned manipulation; we study it for survival in multi-agent reinforcement learning.

Where the hazard sits in the learning problem decides what such a comparison can teach. In some fire-related work the hazard is the task, as for aerial agents rewarded for moving to burning trees on a fire's boundary <d-cite key="haksar2018"></d-cite>; safe reinforcement learning typically places it in a cost or a constraint <d-cite key="garcia2015safe"></d-cite>. In the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE) it does neither. The fire is an *ambient hazard* (Definition 2, §3): a fixed stochastic process with nothing in it optimizing against the agents, carried by no cost or constraint channel and invisible to the reward, which reads task variables and agent deaths and prices every death at $d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->, whatever its cause. Such a hazard is not new in itself; what it gives this study is a comparison in which no reward or cost signal mentions the hazard in either arm. Proposition 1 shows that the fire, which looks like nonstationarity to any one agent, is state: CHE is a well-defined Dec-POMDP, whose states reachable within the horizon form a finite set (§3). With communication denied it does not reduce to a multi-agent POMDP, and finite-horizon Dec-POMDPs are NEXP-complete <d-cite key="bernstein2002"></d-cite>, so we learn policies rather than plan (Remark 1).

Figure 1 shows one episode. CHE is written in pure JAX, and a cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode. For the idealized kernel the burnt region is exactly a bond-percolation cluster (Proposition 2) <d-cite key="grassberger1983"></d-cite><d-cite key="kesten1980"></d-cite>, so the three<!-- N:CAL-3 --> severities are values of the fire's transmissibility β placed below, near and above a *measured* percolation threshold of the implemented kernel (§4). Two stressors are coupled to the fire: collapse can add ignitions (Coupling A, of strength $\kappa_A$; §5), and smoke attenuates each agent's egocentric observation by Beer–Lambert transmittance (Coupling B, $\kappa_B$; §6). A third, link denial at level δ (§7), is independent of it; $\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)$ is the environment configuration. Each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel whose random numbers are drawn whether or not it is on, so switching it off reproduces the nested model's trajectories bit for bit under the same random keys (§3).

The training question becomes a pre-registered test of two training mixtures (Figure 2).

At θ\* the founding hypothesis, that joint training improves both outcomes, did not occur (Figure 3). Joint training raises survival by 0.96<!-- N:RES-2 --> points (Šidák-corrected 95 %<!-- N:DES-13 --> CI [0.47, 1.44]<!-- N:RES-2 -->; points are rate differences × 100), and the survival test rejects. Task completion shows no detectable change, −1.73<!-- N:RES-1 --> points (CI [−4.12, +0.66]<!-- N:RES-1 -->), which excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66<!-- N:RES-1 --> points. The falsifier registered for an asymmetric outcome passes (§9.2), so the asymmetry stands. Under Definition 2 it lies between a penalized channel, deaths, and a rewarded one, the task. Read over training, both co-primaries are **sign unstable over the final half**<!-- N:RES-11 -->: the survival gap opens only in the last fifth<!-- N:RES-12 --> of the budget, and completion leans negative from 70 %<!-- N:RES-13 --> of it onward (Figure 12).

The survival gain **does not survive the inert-share correction**. The fire-only third was registered as a bias, with ISO-4 to remove it, on 2026-09-18<!-- N:DES-15 -->, mid-grid and before unblinding; ISO-4 is ISO without its δ-only components, a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family with 20<!-- N:DES-13 --> seeds. Its survival is 0.98<!-- N:RES-7 --> points above ISO's, and Γ₄, JOINT minus ISO-4, is −0.03<!-- N:RES-6 --> points (Šidák-corrected 95 %<!-- N:DES-13 --> CI [−0.68, +0.63]<!-- N:RES-6 -->), and a same-card secondary replicate, proposed after Γ₄ was seen, agrees (§9.2). As the registration requires, we do not describe the survival advantage as a composition effect. Two limits apply. Γ₄'s upper bound, +0.63<!-- N:RES-6 -->, lies above Γ's lower bound, +0.47<!-- N:RES-2 -->: the correction excludes a large composition effect, not every one. And ISO-4 raises both the active share (from two thirds<!-- N:DES-14 --> to one<!-- N:DES-14 -->) and each coupling's frequency (from a third<!-- N:DES-7 --> to a half<!-- N:DES-14 -->), so its rise cannot say by itself which matters; §9.2 reports what the non-verdict-bearing secondary design adds, its slope on the fire-only share and where its plane fit fails. Once the active share is matched, JOINT's remaining differences add nothing detectable to survival, although it still sees each coupling twice<!-- N:DES-14 --> as often as ISO-4 and always in combination.

The argument is general, though measured in one environment: a joint-versus-isolated comparison that leaves the active share free can report an active-share effect as a composition effect. In the search described in §2, which read abstracts and did not query IEEE Xplore, we found no reinforcement-learning comparison that matches its arms on that share. We claim no convergence guarantee and no theorem that joint training wins: Proposition 4 bounds what isolated training can be *guaranteed* to transfer, not what it does, and Theorem 1 concerns adaptivity against memorization in a two-corridor toy. Nor do we claim an exact critical value for the implemented kernel, or a survival gap robust to the training budget: every comparison is at matched compute, none at convergence.

Our contributions are:

1. **An environment built as an instrument** (§§3–7): a lethal fire confined to the transition and observation kernels (Definition 2), each step factorized into collapse, fire, smoke, agents and communication (Proposition 1), severity by measured dynamical phase, bitwise-exact nested ablations, and theory that doubles as unit tests (Proposition 3, Theorem 1, Remark 2′).
2. **Calibration findings that stand alone** (§§5–7). Under trained policies, collapse-seeded ignitions fall 4.8-fold<!-- N:CA-4 --> from low to high severity, as the Coupling-A lock had recorded from the random-policy calibration before any trained policy was measured (§5), while their share near agents stays flat<!-- N:CA-6 -->. Smoke costs survival at high severity (the same direction in three of three<!-- N:CB-5 --> replicates; masking concentrated 16–118-fold<!-- N:CB-10 --> on moments of danger), but measured −0.03<!-- N:CB-9 --> points at the test's medium severity, graded only against a three-seed<!-- N:CB-9 --> spread. Communication is inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale.
3. **A measurement protocol in which acceptance bars come with floors** (§8): floors per metric, per GPU and per artifact; contrasts graded on their own standard error; design-stage power as the minimum effect detectable with 80 %<!-- N:METH-7 --> power at the family-corrected α; the bars without a floor named.
4. **A pre-registered test of joint against isolated training** (§9), whose survival advantage does not survive a correction registered before unblinding, and a rule whose argument is general: match the active share before attributing a gain to composition.

2 Related work

For each line of work, this section says where it places the hazard and what its comparisons hold fixed. §1 stated what ours holds fixed (Figure 2); how we searched is described at the end.

Where the hazard enters learning. Table 1 sets the nearest fire-related and safety-related work beside CHE.

Table 1. Where the hazard enters the learning or planning problem.

work agents and method where the hazard enters
Haksar & Schwager (2018) [7] aerial agents fighting wildfire; multi-agent deep Q-network the task: a reward for moving to burning trees on the fire's boundary (their Algorithm 2); the paper gives the fire no way to harm the agents
JaxWildfire (Çakır et al., 2025) [14] one air-tanker agent; JAX cellular-automaton wildfire simulator the reward, hazard-priced: a per-step penalty proportional to the number of burning cells, with a bonus for full extinction; harm to the agent is not described
safe reinforcement learning, as García & Fernández (2015) define it [8] learning policies that maximize return while ensuring reasonable system performance and/or respecting safety constraints a constraint or a cost, typically
VULCAN (Liu & Yan, 2026) [15] a proposed approach to multi-robot fire search and rescue, planned by a vision-language model rather than learned a hazard cost map that folds in smoke, heat and sensing uncertainty
CHE (this paper) 12ENV-6 agents; independent PPO ambient: the transition and observation kernels only; no reward, cost or constraint reads the fire, and the reward prices each death at \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause

CHE's reward is hazard-blind rather than hazard-priced (Definition 2): it prices an outcome, each death the same. VULCAN's preliminary evaluation covers baselines, not the approach itself, and its evaluation metric is also abbreviated CHE (cumulative hazard exposure), which is unrelated to our environment's name.

A hazard that feeds on itself. Coupling A lets structural collapse add ignitions. The nearest neighbour we found is Raut et al. (2026) [16], who model public-safety power shutoffs as a single-agent decision problem in which the power flows that follow the operator's switching decisions set the failure probability of lines in wildfire areas, which the authors treat as ignition risk; their reward is negative operating cost, and there is no spatial spread model. CHE's agents influence the hazard too, but weakly and indirectly: an occupied weak cell collapses nine timesCA-8 as often per step as an empty one (Definition 5), yet agent load adds only 0.4–1.4 %CA-9 to the collapses per episode under the trained policies of §5, and 7.4–8.3 %CA-9 under random policies. Since a collapse may seed fire, we do not claim that CHE's hazard grows without agent influence. The difference from Raut et al. is one of directness and size: there the controlled decision sets the failure probability, while in CHE ignition is not an action available to the agents, and where they stand moves the collapse count only slightly.

Perception degraded by the hazard. VULCAN degrades simulated sensing with smoke density and visibility loss, and infers smoke density from image degradation and depth consistency, but has no transmittance model; JaxWildfire's observations are partial and egocentric but not attenuated. In the search described below, we found no reinforcement-learning environment whose observation kernel attenuates by transmittance, as Coupling B does along each line of sight through smoke.

Simulators and benchmarks in JAX. CHE follows JaxMARL's environment conventions [17] and trains with independent PPO [12]. None of the JAX multi-agent benchmarks we surveyed, JaxMARL, Multi-Agent Craftax [18] and Assistax [19], has an evolving, spreading lethal hazard field or perception attenuated by a hazard. Multi-Agent Craftax comes closest in kind: its agents lose health to hunger and enemies and can die, but its lava is static terrain that agents cannot enter. In Crafter and in Craftax-Classic, the JAX reimplementation described alongside Craftax, lava is lethal, and Crafter and Craftax reward each health point regained and penalize each one lost [20][21], pricing an outcome rather than the hazard, much as our reward prices each death. POBAX [22] is a single-agent suite for partial observability, BenchMARL [23] a multi-agent benchmarking library built on TorchRL rather than JAX, and JaxWildfire has a spreading fire in JAX, with one agent.

Choosing a training distribution. Domain randomization trains on a broad, fixed distribution over simulator parameters so that the deployed setting looks like one more sample [24][25]. Unsupervised environment design adapts the distribution to the learner instead: PAIRED generates environments from a regret objective [26], Prioritized Level Replay revisits levels by their estimated learning potential [27] and was later recast as environment design [28], and ACCEL evolves levels near the frontier of the agent's ability [29]. These methods ask which distribution to train on, usually adaptively; we compare two fixed distributions and evaluate no adaptive curriculum (§10). Our held-out test belongs to the broader problem of zero-shot generalization [5].

Compositional generalization over environmental factors. The closest earlier study of our question is Gao et al. (2024) [6]. In visual imitation learning for robotic manipulation, they ask whether policies compose environmental factors (object type and position, table texture and height, camera position) to succeed on unseen combinations, and use the answer to design data collection. They compare strategies, among them one that varies a single factor at a time from a base setting and one that cycles through factors so that more combinations appear, at a matched number of demonstrations and across levels of a rough effort proxy, the number of factor changes (their §III-C and §IV-A). Policies do compose, more weakly when both factors are physical (their §V-B), and the strategy that covers more combinations does better. Their design is built for data efficiency, not to separate co-occurrence from how often each factor value appears; ours matches compute and, in the ISO-4 correction, the share of training that carries any stressor. The settings differ too: their factors change what the robot sees and must do, and their policies are learned by imitation, while two of our stressors are causally coupled to a lethal hazard and our policies are learned by multi-agent reinforcement learning. CompoSuite [30] studies compositional multi-task reinforcement learning over robots, objects, objectives and obstacles: composition of tasks, not of co-occurring stressors. The DBCA method [13] builds splits whose atom distributions are similar and whose compound distributions differ. Our secondary sweep follows that idea, holding each coupling's frequency fixed while co-occurrence varies; our ISO and JOINT endpoints do not, since JOINT sees each coupling three timesDES-7 as often as ISO (§1, §9).

Mixed and simultaneous perturbations. Robust reinforcement learning asks the same question about perturbations. Erdem & Üre (2025) [31] train single-agent continuous-control policies against a learned adversary that perturbs observations, actions, or both, at every step (their §3.4 and Algorithm 1). They report that agents trained against one modality remain vulnerable to simultaneous attacks on both (their Tables 3–4), and agents trained against their balanced mixed adversary do better under single-type attacks than those trained against a naive mixed adversary (their Table 5). No arm trains on a mixture of single-type attacks: each single-type arm never meets the other modality, and the mixed arms carry the combined budget, which the authors note is more severe. Every adversarially trained arm is perturbed throughout, so nothing in their design corresponds to the fire-only share our correction removes. What it bundles (per-modality exposure, co-occurrence and a larger budget) is nearer to what our corrected contrast leaves in, where JOINT still sees each coupling twiceDES-14 as often as ISO-4, though their single-type arms see the other modality not at all. Their result is therefore not a test at matched per-modality exposure, and it neither supports nor contradicts our corrected null. Agrawal et al. (2023) [32] train multi-agent policies against random reward, state and action noise, one or two noise types at a time, with a curriculum that raises the noise level and sets training length, so exposure differs between their models. In supervised vision, Tramèr & Boneh (2019) [33] and Hsiung et al. (2023) [34] study robustness to multiple and to composite perturbations.

Theory. Chen et al. (2022) [35] model domain randomization as returning the optimal history-dependent policy of a latent MDP, a set of fully observed MDPs that differ in their transitions. Every upper bound they prove on its shortfall against the optimal policy of the true MDP assumes each MDP is communicating, with bounded diameter; without that assumption, their Proposition 1 shows the shortfall can grow linearly in the horizon, through an irreversible choice made before anything distinguishes the candidate MDPs. Theorem 1 here (§6) is kin to that construction but not covered by it. Its no-information endpoint has the structure of their Proposition 1's uninformed commitment, with two branches. But its two-corridor toy, with the signals encoded as states, has an absorbing failure, so their diameter assumption fails and their upper bounds do not apply; it has a single terminal reward and can reveal the environment before the branch, which their Proposition 1 does not address; and it measures a different gap, the adaptive optimum against the policy optimal for a fixed map rather than against the optimum for a known environment. Chen et al. therefore do not subsume Theorem 1, and Theorem 1 does not extend their bounds. Pynadath & Tambe (2002) [36] relate the complexity of team decision problems with communication to observability: free communication lowers the complexity when the world is collectively observable (their Theorem 4 and Table 2), and makes no difference when it is individually observable or not observable at all (their Theorems 5–6). Remark 2′ (§7) refines this for collective partial observability: communication's marginal value vanishes when interchangeable agents' views are redundant, and returns when roles are irreplaceable or deaths are costly. The survey by Hu et al. (2025, revised 2026) [37] of adaptability in multi-agent reinforcement learning calls an environment family highly diagnostic if it "systematically varies" factors such as population, observability or communication structure "while holding other factors fixed"; it mentions compositional generalization but does not analyse shifts in several factors at once.

How we searched. We searched with LLM-based research assistants between August and October 2026DX-101: first a census of benchmark venues and a search for theorems that could subsume ours, then a search of domain-randomization, environment-design, multi-task and robust-learning neighbours, both through web search and the arXiv API, reading abstracts. The works this section describes in detail were then read in full; the remaining citations rest on their abstracts. We did not query IEEE Xplore. Wherever this paper says that no work does something, it means no work found by this search.

Kit source of this part sections/02_related_work.md

## 2 Related work

For each line of work, this section says where it places the hazard and what its comparisons hold fixed. §1 stated what ours holds fixed (Figure 2); how we searched is described at the end.

**Where the hazard enters learning.** Table 1 sets the nearest fire-related and safety-related work beside CHE.

**Table 1.** Where the hazard enters the learning or planning problem.

| work | agents and method | where the hazard enters |
|---|---|---|
| Haksar & Schwager (2018) <d-cite key="haksar2018"></d-cite> | aerial agents fighting wildfire; multi-agent deep Q-network | **the task**: a reward for moving to burning trees on the fire's boundary (their Algorithm 2); the paper gives the fire no way to harm the agents |
| JaxWildfire (Çakır et al., 2025) <d-cite key="cakir2025jaxwildfire"></d-cite> | one air-tanker agent; JAX cellular-automaton wildfire simulator | **the reward**, hazard-priced: a per-step penalty proportional to the number of burning cells, with a bonus for full extinction; harm to the agent is not described |
| safe reinforcement learning, as García & Fernández (2015) define it <d-cite key="garcia2015safe"></d-cite> | learning policies that maximize return while ensuring reasonable system performance and/or respecting safety constraints | **a constraint or a cost**, typically |
| VULCAN (Liu & Yan, 2026) <d-cite key="liu2026vulcan"></d-cite> | a proposed approach to multi-robot fire search and rescue, planned by a vision-language model rather than learned | **a hazard cost map** that folds in smoke, heat and sensing uncertainty |
| CHE (this paper) | 12<!-- N:ENV-6 --> agents; independent PPO | **ambient**: the transition and observation kernels only; no reward, cost or constraint reads the fire, and the reward prices each death at $d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->, whatever its cause |

CHE's reward is hazard-blind rather than hazard-priced (Definition 2): it prices an outcome, each death the same. VULCAN's preliminary evaluation covers baselines, not the approach itself, and its evaluation metric is also abbreviated CHE (cumulative hazard exposure), which is unrelated to our environment's name.

3 The environment

CHE is a grid world in which a team of agents forages while a fire burns. This section states the environment formally, pairs each statement with the code that implements it, and gives the one instance used in every reported experiment. Figure 4 shows one environment step.

Five kernels sampled from s to s′ in the order of Proposition 1: collapse, fire, smoke, agents, communication. Coupling A (thick orange arrow) carries the collapse increment c′ − c into the fire kernel; Coupling B (thick blue arrow) carries the smoke field ρ′ into the observation kernel; the denial level δ enters the communication kernel, which has no hazard input. Each agent observes a 9 × 9ENV-6 egocentric crop of the post-step state. The agents kernel reads the post-step fields, TX(x, a, h′, c′), as the code and Definition 1 do. The arrow colours belong to this figure only; in the interactive figures orange is burning fire and blue marks agents. The reward is not drawn (Definition 2). Status: schematic, no data · Interact: none (static figure).

3.1 State, kernel and step order

Definition 1 (Compound Hostile Environment). A CHE is a Dec-POMDP \(\mathcal{M}_\theta = \langle I, \mathcal{S}, \{\mathcal{A}_i\}, T_\theta, R, \{\Omega_i\}, O_\theta, \gamma \rangle\) with a finite set of agents \(I\) on a grid \(G\), action sets \(\mathcal{A}_i\), observation sets \(\Omega_i\), reward \(R\) (Definition 2), observation kernel \(O_\theta\) and discount \(\gamma\). It is indexed by the environment configuration \(\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)\): the fire's per-neighbour ignition probability \(\beta\), the strengths \(\kappa_A\) and \(\kappa_B\) of Couplings A and B, and the communication-denial level \(\delta\). Its state space factors as

\[\mathcal{S} = \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{H} \times \mathcal{P} \times \mathcal{C} \times \mathcal{K} \times \{0, \dots, T\},\]

where \(x \in \mathcal{X}\) holds the agents' positions \(x_i \in G\), their alive flags \(\alpha_i \in \{0, 1\}\) and the task state; \(h \in \mathcal{H} = \{\mathrm{Fuel}, \mathrm{Burning}, \mathrm{Burnt}\}^G\) is the fire; \(\rho \in \mathcal{P} = \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^G\) is the smoke field; \(c \in \mathcal{C} = \{\mathrm{intact}, \mathrm{collapsed}\}^G\) is the structure; \(k \in \mathcal{K}\) is the realized communication graph on \(I\); and the last factor is the step index \(t\), with horizon \(T\). The transition kernel factorizes as

\[\begin{aligned} T_\theta(s' \mid s, a) = {} & \underbrace{T_C(c' \mid c, x)}_{\mathrm{collapse}} \; \underbrace{T_H^{\beta,\kappa_A}(h' \mid h, c, c')}_{\mathrm{fire;\ Coupling\ A}} \; \underbrace{T_\rho(\rho' \mid \rho, h')}_{\mathrm{smoke}} \\ & \times \underbrace{T_X(x' \mid x, a, h', c')}_{\mathrm{agents}} \; \underbrace{T_K^{\delta}(k' \mid x')}_{\mathrm{comms}}, \end{aligned}\]

and agent \(i\)'s observation is drawn from the post-step state, \(o'_i \sim O^i_{\kappa_B}(\cdot \mid x', h', \rho', c', k')\). The dependence of \(T_H\) on the collapse increment \(c' - c\) is Coupling A (§5); the dependence of \(O^i_{\kappa_B}\) on the fire, through the smoke field \(\rho'\) it produces, is Coupling B (§6). The weak-cell terrain that \(T_C\) reads is drawn at reset and fixed for the episode (§5), and a training mixture (§9.1) draws \(\theta\) once per episode, at reset. On notation: an unsubscripted \(\gamma\) is always this discount, while the subscripted \(\gamma_p\) and \(\gamma_n\) of §9 are the secondary design's slopes; Corollary 1 (§4) writes its critical exponents as numbers.

Proposition 1 (Well-posedness and Markov closure). For every \(\theta\), the process \(s_t = (x_t, h_t, \rho_t, c_t, k_t, t)\) under any joint policy is a Markov chain, and \(\mathcal{M}_\theta\) is a well-defined Dec-POMDP: finite-horizon optimal joint policies exist, and the standard value definitions apply.

Proof. Each factor of \(T_\theta\) is a conditional kernel whose conditioning variables are components of \((s, a)\) or components of \(s'\) already sampled within the same step: \(c' \sim T_C(\cdot \mid c, x)\), then \(h' \sim T_H(\cdot \mid h, c, c')\), then \(\rho' = T_\rho(\rho, h')\) (deterministic), then \(x' \sim T_X(\cdot \mid x, a, h', c')\), then \(k' \sim T_K(\cdot \mid x')\). A sequential composition of Markov kernels is a Markov kernel on the product space, so \(T_\theta\) is a valid transition kernel on \(\mathcal{S}\) and \((s_t)\) is Markov. The action sets are finite. The state space itself is not, since \(\mathcal{P} = \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^G\) is not a finite set; but every stochastic factor and the reset distribution have finite support, and \(\rho\) evolves deterministically from \(\rho_0 = 0\), so the states and observations reachable within the horizon form finite sets; restricted to them, \(\mathcal{M}_\theta\) is a finite Dec-POMDP, so optimal finite-horizon joint policies exist by the standard argument. \(\blacksquare\)

From one agent's point of view the fire looks like nonstationarity. Proposition 1 says that formally it is state, evolving under a stationary kernel, so the standard value definitions apply.

Remark 1 (Hardness). Bernstein et al. (2002)[9] show that finite-horizon Dec-POMDPs are NEXP-complete. With communication denied (\(\delta > 0\)), a CHE does not reduce to a multi-agent POMDP, so this hardness applies; it is why we learn policies rather than plan.

Listing 1, the body of the environment's step function, follows the factorization line by line: \(c'\) from the pre-step occupancy; \(h'\) as the fire's spread followed by the Coupling-A impulse from \(c' - c\); \(\rho'\) from \(h'\); the agents against \(h'\) and \(c'\); and the link graph from the new positions and alive flags, on its own stream, with no hazard argument. The reward line reads the task reward and the two death counts and nothing else. (tl is the episode's \(\theta\); the frozen branch is an early control that no configuration in this paper uses.) The observation is then drawn from the new state (che/env/env.py:394-397).

Listing 1. One environment step in the order of Proposition 1.

# che/env/env.py:298-358 (verbatim)
    k_struct, k_seed, k_fire = jax.random.split(key, 3)

    # 1. c' ~ T_C(c, x): reads *pre-step* occupancy.
    occ_pre = occupancy_grid(state.agent_pos, state.agent_alive, cfg.grid_size)
    structure_new = structure_step(
        k_struct,
        state.structure,
        state.weak,
        occ_pre.astype(jnp.float32),
        lambda_0=th.lambda_0,
        lambda_load=th.lambda_load,
    )
    collapse_increment = (structure_new == COLLAPSED) & (state.structure == INTACT)

    # 2. h' ~ T_H(h, c, c'): CA spread, then the Coupling A impulse from the
    # collapse increment (seeded cells are Burning in h', spread next step).
    seed_mask = coupling_a_seed_mask(
        k_seed, collapse_increment, kappa_A=tl.kappa_A, r_seed=th.r_seed
    )
    hazard_ca = hazard_step(k_fire, state.hazard, beta=tl.beta, iota=th.iota)
    if cfg.hazard_mode == "frozen":
        # M1.3: h is frozen — the CA/seed draws above still happen and are
        # discarded, so dynamic<->frozen with the same key share every
        # other stream bitwise (invariant #3). Frozen Burning cells stay
        # Burning: lethal and smoke-emitting, but never spread or burn out.
        hazard_new = state.hazard
        seeded_ignitions = jnp.zeros_like(seed_mask)
    else:
        hazard_new = seed_ignitions(hazard_ca, seed_mask)
        seeded_ignitions = hazard_ca != hazard_new  # Fuel cells Coupling A lit

    # 3. rho' from h' (Def. 6; smoke outlives flame).
    smoke_new = smoke_step(state.smoke, hazard_new, sigma_s=th.sigma_s, eta=th.eta)

    # 4. x' ~ T_X + task dynamics. M1.1: lethality/blocking against h'/c'.
    # DECISION: an agent disabled this step does not collect food this step
    # (it died before/on arrival) — occupancy filters on the post-death
    # alive vector, keeping "dead agents never collect" exact.
    pos_new, alive_new, deaths_fire, deaths_collapse, blocked_moves = agent_step(
        state.agent_pos,
        state.agent_alive,
        actions,
        hazard_new,
        structure_new,
        collapse_increment,
        cfg.grid_size,
    )
    occ_post = occupancy_grid(pos_new, alive_new, cfg.grid_size)
    food_new, task_reward = task_step(state.food, occ_post)
    # Def.-2-compliant death penalty: reads only the alpha transition (an X
    # variable) — never hazard/smoke/structure directly.
    reward = task_reward - th.death_penalty * (deaths_fire + deaths_collapse).astype(
        jnp.float32
    )

    # 5. k' ~ T_K(x'): comms channel (Def. 7, M5.0). Reads post-step
    # positions/aliveness only — never h', rho' or c' — and draws from its
    # own fold_in stream, so delta cannot perturb any kernel above.
    links, links_in_range = _comms_obs(
        jax.random.fold_in(key, _COMMS_STREAM), pos_new, alive_new, cfg, tl.delta
    )

3.2 The fire and its smoke

Definition 3 (Fire kernel). Each cell is Fuel, Burning or Burnt. At each step, independently for each ordered pair of a Burning cell \(g\) and a Fuel von Neumann neighbour \(g' \in N(g)\), the cell \(g'\) ignites with probability \(\beta\). A Burning cell becomes Burnt after exactly one step, and Burnt is absorbing. Fuel cells may also ignite spontaneously at rate \(\iota \ge 0\) per step; CHE uses \(\iota = 0\)DX-1.

Every episode starts from an all-Fuel grid with one Burning cell at a uniformly random position (che/env/env.py:241-243). On every step the kernel draws one uniform per cell and incoming direction; these slots correspond one-to-one to the ordered pairs \(g \to g'\), so per-pair independence is exact. Because a Burning cell is Burnt one step later, each unordered edge sees at most one ignition attempt in a trajectory, so these uniforms realize the per-edge Bernoulli variables of Proposition 2's coupling exactly (che/env/hazard.py:8-16). Cells outside the arena never burn; there is no wraparound.

Proposition 2 (Bond percolation). Under Definition 3 with \(\iota = 0\) and a single ignition at the origin, the set of eventually-Burnt cells is equal in distribution to the open cluster of the origin in i.i.d. bond percolation on \((G, E)\) with edge-open probability \(\beta\).

Here \(E\) is the set of von Neumann edges of the arena. The proof is a short coupling (Appendix A); it is the correspondence between an epidemic with a constant infectious period and bond percolation described by Grassberger (1983)[10], and it is what lets §4 define severity by percolation phase. Its hypotheses hold for CHE's fire on its own. With Coupling A on, collapses can add ignitions during the episode, and the burnt set is then no longer one origin's cluster.

Smoke outlives flame. Per cell,

\[\rho' = e^{-\eta}\rho + \sigma_s \mathbf{1}[h' = \mathrm{Burning}],\]

with emission \(\sigma_s = 1.0\) and decay \(\eta = 0.5\), held fixed by convention and never calibratedDX-1. The update reads the post-step fire \(h'\), so a cell emits \(\sigma_s\) once, in the step it burns, and its smoke then decays geometrically; there is no spatial smoothing (che/env/hazard.py:67-79). The smoke field is state, not what an agent sees: an agent sees only its crop of the state, attenuated by Coupling B through \(\rho'\).

Three stressors attach to these kernels, one parameter each: Coupling A (\(\kappa_A\), Definition 5, §5) seeds the fire from new collapses, Coupling B (\(\kappa_B\), Definition 6, §6) attenuates each agent's crop through \(\rho'\), and communication denial (\(\delta\), Definition 7, §7) thins the link graph that \(T_K\) samples from positions and alive flags only.

3.3 Agents, deaths and a hazard-blind reward

The agents kernel \(T_X(x' \mid x, a, h', c')\) acts in three deterministic stages (che/env/env.py:159-196). An alive agent on a cell that collapses in this step falls and is disabled before it moves. Survivors take one of 5ENV-8 actions, stay or a von Neumann move clipped to the grid; a move into a collapsed cell is cancelled, and Burnt cells are passable. Then an agent whose new cell is Burning in \(h'\) is disabled, so a cell igniting under a standing agent kills it; Burning cells can be entered, and avoidance must be learned. A disabled agent stays disabled, holds still, collects nothing and vanishes from the others' observations. Death consumes no random numbers, so it cannot shift another kernel's stream.

Definition 2 (Ambient hazard). The hazard is ambient if and only if (i) and (ii) hold.

(i) Reward independence. The reward is a function of task variables and of the agents' alive transition only,

\[R(s,a,s') = R_{\mathrm{task}}(\mathrm{task}(x), a, \mathrm{task}(x')) - d_p\,\lvert\{i : \alpha_i = 1,\ \alpha'_i = 0\}\rvert ,\]

where \(\mathrm{task}(x)\) denotes the task variables (in CHE, the food grid and the alive agents' positions) and \(d_p \ge 0\) is a fixed death penalty; no term of \(R\) references \(h\), \(\rho\) or \(c\), and the hazard appears in no auxiliary cost or constraint channel: no CMDP constraint, no Lagrangian penalty, no safety critic.

(ii) Non-adversarial. \(T_H\) is a fixed stochastic kernel: nothing in the environment optimizes, learns or best-responds.

Under clause (i), survival is learned through two hazard-blind routes: a dead agent collects no further task reward, and each death costs a fixed \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause, in every configuration this paper reports. Fire and collapse affect return only through the transition and observation kernels, never through the reward: the transition kernel disables agents and, for collapse, blocks moves; the observation kernel shows them to the agents, and through it Coupling B attenuates perception. The penalty prices an outcome, not the hazard. A hazard-priced reward signals the hazard's state whether or not anything is harmed: in JaxWildfire, for example, Çakır et al. (2025)[14] penalize the agent at every step in proportion to the number of burning cells. CHE's reward carries information about the fire only through outcomes the task also suffers. At \(d_p = 0\) only the first route would remain.

Listing 2 is the task reward: its signature admits the food and occupancy grids and nothing else, so no fire, smoke or structure argument can reach it. Listing 1's reward line subtracts \(d_p\) times the sum of fire and collapse deaths, adding the two causes before pricing them.

Listing 2. The task reward reads food and occupancy only.

# che/env/tasks.py:31-43 (verbatim)
def task_step(food: jax.Array, occupancy: jax.Array) -> tuple[jax.Array, jax.Array]:
    """Collection: an occupied food cell yields +1 team reward and empties.

    Multiple agents on one food cell still collect a single item (+1).
    Returns (food', team_reward). Reads task variables and agent occupancy
    only — no hazard/smoke/structure arguments, by design (Def. 2).
    """
    chex.assert_type(food, jnp.uint8)
    chex.assert_equal_shape([food, occupancy])
    collected = (food == 1) & occupancy
    reward = collected.sum().astype(jnp.float32)
    food_new = jnp.where(collected, jnp.uint8(0), food).astype(jnp.uint8)
    return food_new, reward

Two tests check clause (i). The reward-independence test steps states that differ only in fire, smoke or structure, with every fire and structure difference far from all agents so that the alive transition is pinned equal, and asserts identical rewards, with and without the death penalty (che/tests/test_reward_independence.py). It shows that the reward reads no hazard state directly, which is all such a test can show, since the fire is meant to change return through deaths. The cause-blindness test kills a standing agent by fire in one configuration and by collapse in another, with identical task state, and asserts that both steps return \(-d_p\) (che/tests/test_lethality.py:143-162).

Clause (i) also fixes how the two co-primary outcomes of §9 enter learning. Survival is a penalized channel, each death costing \(d_p\); completion is a rewarded channel, each collected item earning +1ENV-9; neither mentions the fire. An asymmetric result between them is an asymmetry between a penalized channel and a rewarded one, and §9.2 reads it in those terms.

The record holds one ablation of \(d_p\), from the calibration phase, and we report it with its scope: the fire alone with no coupling on, 500 updatesENV-2, 3 seeds per cellENV-2, an observation encoding that predates the one used here, and means over the final training updates. Raising \(d_p\) from 0 to 0.5ENV-1 moved High survival from 0.575 to 0.866ENV-2 and High completion from 0.765 to 0.821ENV-3, moved Medium survival from 0.931 to 0.951 with completion 0.750 at bothENV-4, and left Low tied, 0.991 against 0.992ENV-5. It was not re-run under the current observation or with the couplings.

Clause (ii) does not make the hazard independent of the agents. An agent standing on a weak cell raises that cell's per-step collapse probability ninefoldCA-8, and a collapse can seed fire. Most collapses happen with no agent present: agent load adds 0.4–1.4 %CA-9 to the number of collapses per episode under trained policies (Phase-3 policies, 500 updatesCA-9) and 7.4–8.3 %CA-9 under random ones. The environment is non-adversarial in the formal sense, a fixed kernel with no objective, but it is not agent-independent (§2).

3.4 Nested models: ablations are exact by construction

Each stressor is a parameter of one kernel, and setting it to zero recovers a nested special case of the same model, never a different environment.

stressor parameter lives in off (\(=0\)) recovers asserted by
Coupling A \(\kappa_A\) \(T_H\), through \(c' - c\) a fire blind to the structure che/tests/test_nesting.py
Coupling B \(\kappa_B\) \(O^i\) perception independent of the hazard Listing 3
communication denial \(\delta\) \(T_K\) free, range-limited communication che/tests/test_comms.py

The fire's own \(\beta\) is a parameter of the same kind, but it is positive in every configuration, and its value is set by severity relative to the measured threshold (§4).

One engineering rule makes the nesting exact: every stochastic branch consumes its random stream unconditionally, drawing its uniforms and comparing them against a probability that may be zero; no draw is gated on a parameter's value. Coupling B's reveal draw, the link draw and the per-episode mixture draw each use a stream derived from the reset or step key by a fixed tag, so adding them left the existing streams untouched. Setting a stressor to zero changes comparisons, never which random numbers are drawn, so the same keys reproduce the nested model's trajectory bit for bit, with no re-implementation or re-tuning of a separate environment.

Listing 3, the test for Coupling B, runs the environment at \(\kappa_B = 0\) with the masking observation and with the observation that predates masking, under the same keys, and asserts bitwise-identical fire, smoke, structure, food, positions, alive flags and rewards. Stepping both by hand, it then asserts identical content planes, an all-ones visibility plane and a zero masked share.

Listing 3. With \(\kappa_B = 0\), the environment is bitwise the one without Coupling B.

# che/tests/test_coupling_b.py:131-156 (verbatim)
def test_kappa_b_zero_bitwise_recovers_obs_v2():
    """Nesting (M4.1): obs v3 at kappa_B = 0 is the pre-masking env plus a
    constant all-ones visibility plane — state trajectories, rewards, and
    content planes bitwise identical to obs v2 under the same keys."""
    theta = dataclasses.replace(_HOT, kappa_B=0.0)
    cfg3 = EnvConfig(grid_size=L, n_agents=4, horizon=64, theta=theta)
    cfg2 = dataclasses.replace(cfg3, obs_version=2)
    a = _traj(theta, cfg=cfg3)
    b = _traj(theta, cfg=cfg2)
    _assert_bitwise_equal(
        a, b, ("hazard", "smoke", "structure", "food", "pos", "alive", "reward")
    )
    # Obs-level recovery over a hand-stepped rollout with shared keys.
    key = jax.random.PRNGKey(3)
    key, k_reset = jax.random.split(key)
    obs3, s3 = reset(k_reset, cfg3)
    obs2, s2 = reset(k_reset, cfg2)
    for _ in range(20):
        assert (obs3["grid"][..., :7] == obs2["grid"]).all()
        assert (obs3["grid"][..., 7] == 1.0).all()
        key, k_act, k_step = jax.random.split(key, 3)
        actions = jax.random.randint(k_act, (4,), 0, 5, jnp.int32)
        obs3, s3, _, _, info3 = step(k_step, s3, actions, cfg3)
        obs2, s2, _, _, _ = step(k_step, s2, actions, cfg2)
        assert float(info3["masked_frac"]) == 0.0
    assert (s3.smoke > 0).any()  # the fire actually made smoke

The other two nestings have tests of the same kind. With \(\kappa_A = 0\), collapse activity leaves the fire and smoke trajectories bitwise unchanged (che/tests/test_nesting.py); at \(\delta = 0\), the environment reproduces a trajectory digest recorded before the communication channel existed (che/tests/test_comms.py). Because a gate such as if kappa_A > 0: around a draw on a dedicated stream would be invisible to behavioural tests, structural tests assert that the traced computation still contains its random-number primitives at parameter value zero. These tests run in the CPU suite; the digest is checked on the CPU only, since the smoke exponential is not bit-identical across backends.

3.5 The instance, training and metrics

Every reported experiment uses one instance: a 64 × 64 grid, 12 agents, a horizon of 256 steps, a 9 × 9 egocentric crop and 32 food itemsENV-6. Food is scattered on distinct cells at reset and does not respawn; agents start on uniformly random cells. An alive agent on a food cell collects it, for a team reward of +1 per itemENV-9. The observation has 8 channelsENV-7: six indicators (burning, burnt, food, weak, collapsed, alive occupancy), the raw smoke density, and Coupling B's visibility plane, which records which cells were revealed so that unseen can be told from absent; an agent's own cell is always revealed. An own-state vector gives the agent's row and column divided by the side \(L\), its alive flag, and \(t\) divided by the horizonENV-7.

All agents share one actor-critic network, which reads the agent's crop, its own-state vector and the aggregate of messages delivered to it: two convolutions with 16 and 32 channels, a dense layer of 128 units, and separate actor and critic heads with a 64-unit layer eachENV-10. A message head emits a vector that neighbours receive one step later; it receives no gradient, so it emits a frozen-at-initialization projection of trained trunk features (§7).

Training is the independent PPO of de Witt et al. (2020)[12], implemented in the PureJaxRL style of Lu et al. (2022)[38] on the environment conventions of JaxMARL, Rutherford et al. (2024)[17]: one shared-parameter policy per training seedENV-11, with each agent's transitions treated as independent samples that all receive the team reward. Each update collects 128 steps from 256 parallel environmentsDX-2, 32,768 environment stepsDX-3, each advancing all 12 agentsENV-6. A run lasts T* = 1000 updatesDES-13 (a training budget, not the episode horizon \(T\)), 32,768,000 environment stepsDX-3, identical for every arm of §9. The other hyperparameters, the same for every run, are in Appendix B.

Two per-episode metrics are the co-primary outcomes of §9. Completion is \(1 - F/32\)ENV-6, where \(F\) is the number of food items remaining at the episode's last step. Survival rate is the fraction of the 12 agentsENV-6 alive at the last step. An episode ends only at the horizon, so an episode in which every agent has died still runs to it (che/env/env.py:361; che/eval/harness.py:44-45). Deaths are also recorded by cause, fire or collapse, as diagnostics; the reward does not distinguish them.
